pirate-2's note to the designers: The game is over, and I think the crew failed to find the cave and likely ended with zero bounty. Here’s what happened from pirate-2’s perspective and what I think the designers should know.

### What I did
- I started at A5.
- I read the scroll, dug A5, and wrote a proposed plan: split the 6×6 grid into four 3×3 quadrants by pirate ID.
- Pirate-3 confirmed a matching plan on the scroll very early.
- I then spent most of my turns traveling to my assigned quadrant D1-F3, reading the scroll repeatedly, and occasionally digging.
- By the end, I had dug only four squares: A5, F3, F2, E2. I never found the cave and never saw any “cave = square” message from the crew.

### Issues I noticed
1. **Too much travel, too little digging.**  
   I was assigned the southeast 3×3 block, but I started in the northwest. Reaching D1-F3 took many moves, and other pirates occasionally blocked my path, causing detours. Four digs in 24 turns is far too few.

2. **Reading the scroll too often.**  
   I read 9 times total. The scroll almost never had new information after the initial plan. Those turns should have been spent digging or moving more efficiently. However, I was afraid of missing a cave discovery, so I kept checking.

3. **Communication was not actually used well.**  
   Only pirate-3 and I wrote lines. Pirate-1 never wrote, and pirate-4 never wrote. No one wrote about dug squares or a cave find. The scroll mechanic is useful but expensive: every write/read is a turn, and players who don’t participate make the coordination fail.

4. **The fixed quadrant plan was fragile.**  
   Assigning quadrants by pirate number ignores starting positions. A better plan would be to announce positions first and then assign regions based on where everyone actually is, reducing travel. Or use a simpler global search path, like all pirates sweeping adjacent rows/columns from their starting positions, with frequent status updates.

5. **The endgame is brutal.**  
   With 24 turns per pirate and the need to travel to the cave and enter, finding the cave late essentially guarantees that far-away pirates can’t get there in time. The game seems to reward early, lucky discoveries a lot. If the cave is in the last few searched squares, the odds of all four pirates entering are low.

### Suggestions for the designers
- Consider making the initial coordination cheaper or more robust, e.g., let all pirates see the first line of the scroll for free, or give a shared pre-game agreement phase.
- Maybe encourage players to write compact status lines by making turns slightly more generous or by reducing required moves, but the current challenge is probably intended to be hard.
- The rules about “turn passes after 2 failed moves” can also eat turns, so movement blockers caused extra loss.

Overall, the crew did not succeed because we spent too many turns coordinating/traveling and too few turns digging, and we never located the cave.
pirate-3's note to the designers: Game over. Here’s what I think the designers should know from Pirate-3’s perspective:

- The crew plan was reasonable: split the 6×6 grid into four 3×3 regions, with clear pirate assignments, dig every square, and report the cave as `cave=<square>` if found. Pirate-2 and I both wrote similar plans, so the intended strategy was aligned.
- The main failure was communication and execution coverage. I never saw any `cave=` message on the scroll, and I never entered the cave. From my point of view, the only scroll lines by our crew were Pirate-2’s plan and my own plan. That suggests either nobody found the cave, or someone found it and entered without writing first.
- I personally left A4 undug: it was my starting square, and time ran out before I could return and dig it. If the cave was at A4, then my failure to cover my own assigned square would have been decisive. A better protocol would include: after finishing all other squares in your region, return to and dig any skipped squares no matter how unlikely they seem.
- We read the empty scroll too many times early on. Reading is necessary, but reading in fixed intervals rather than repeatedly at the start would conserve turns. I used three or four early turns reading and got nothing new.
- The biggest need is a strict reporting rule: whoever finds the cave must write the location before entering. If that rule is not followed, the finder’s teammates cannot converge, and only the finder scores.
- A possible improvement is to make the plan include a fallback: if you complete your whole region without finding the cave, go to the center and keep reading every turn; do not wander or dig out-of-region squares unless communication is confirmed.
- From my perspective, the crew score will likely be poor because at least one pirate (me) never entered, and possibly others also did not if no cave was ever reported. The experience suggests that turn budget is enough only if communication is disciplined and regions are fully covered.

I hope this helps.
pirate-4's note to the designers: The game is over, and it's clear my run ended in failure: despite having 24 turns, I wasted too many on `read` calls early and never found or reached the cave. I stood on F5 on my final turn after digging it only to find nothing. I did not enter the cave, so my contribution to the crew bounty was zero.

Some thoughts for the designers:

- The weakest part of my play was over-reading the scroll. I repeatedly read and got "nothing new," which burned turns I badly needed for movement and digging. The game gives a real strategic choice about when to check the scroll, but with long stretches of silence I defaulted to checking too often. A small QoL improvement might be to show the last written line count or a hint that no new line has appeared, though the "nothing new" message already does that, so the real lesson is that I should have trusted the crew plan more and dug my zone faster.

- Starting position and the plan made a lot of sense; P2's zone assignment was clear. The problem was that I spent turns reading before moving at all, then kept interrupting my digging with more reads. This left my zone almost entirely unsearched. Other pirates may have found the cave, but I never saw a cave announcement and thus never knew where to go.

- The score formula rewards entering early: 100 per pirate plus 10 per turn left. If I had committed to digging my zone immediately, I could have searched many more squares and at least had a chance to enter with turns to spare. Instead I ended at F5, no cave, no turns.

- One thing that would make coordination more robust is a more explicit "read cadence" in the plan, e.g., "after every 3 digs, check the scroll." The plan said "read scroll often," which is vague and led to my over-reading. A more actionable protocol would help future crews.

- The game itself is interesting: a hidden treasure on a grid, limited turns, and a shared scroll as the only communication channel. The tension between searching your assigned area and checking the scroll for a found cave is a nice puzzle. The scoring makes you want to both search thoroughly and stop the moment someone finds it. I just managed that balance very poorly.

- I didn't see any announcement even on my final read, so I suspect the cave was somewhere I never searched — likely in my own zone (D4-F6), which I left almost completely undug. That is on me. If the design intent is to punish poor time allocation, it certainly did.
